## management-governance — module synthesis

# Management-Governance Module — ORCL (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Oracle's leadership is not yet a trustworthy steward of shareholder capital, though the record is mixed, not uniformly bad. Capital allocation swung from real per-share gains through FY2023 (diluted shares down 34.4%) to value-destructive lately: the dividend and much of AI-capex now run on $115.8bn of new debt and equity, not operating cash, alongside a 54% one-year debt jump and an S&P downgrade to BBB-. No 2026 proxy exists yet, so incentive alignment cannot be judged for either co-CEO, and the one disclosed pay metric rewards revenue, not per-share value. Founder Ellison's 40.21% stake is a real alignment anchor, but every named executive sold stock this year, and an unresolved lawsuit alleges the AI-deal outlook was overstated. Verdict: misaligned or weak stewardship.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict:** Misaligned or weak stewardship
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** N — `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` found no triggered hard disqualifier (audit qualification, going concern, promoter pledging >50%, related-party >25%, repeated auditor changes, material restatement, or regulatory enforcement). It separately routes an unresolved securities class action (filed 2026-02-03) as a soft integrity signal for this module to weigh, not a lock — this module carries that signal forward as RF-MGT-005.
- Management quality /100: **57**
- Capital allocation /100: **40**
- Incentive alignment /100: **37**
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **63**
- Disclosure candor /100: **60** (capped — see Section 4)
- Governance risk /100 *(higher = worse)*: **58**
- Data quality /100: **62**
- Overall usefulness /100: **68**
- Insider ownership (one line): Founder/Executive Chair Lawrence Ellison holds 40.21% (~$168.5bn), built almost entirely through option exercise-and-retain rather than open-market cash purchase; every named executive's trailing-12-month Form 4 is a sale, with zero open-market buys by Ellison or either co-CEO on record at any point [`04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`].
- Biggest governance signal (one line): Two CFO-seat changes in eight months and zero disclosed compensation metrics landed in the exact fiscal year Oracle's debt rose 54% to $167.4bn, free cash flow turned to -$24.5bn, and an unresolved securities class action alleges management overstated the AI-deal growth outlook that is funding all of it.
- **Governance Score /100** — `0.20×40 (CapAlloc) + 0.18×37 (Incentive) + 0.18×63 (ShFriendliness) + 0.16×60 (Candor) + 0.16×57 (MgmtQuality) + 0.12×(100−58) (GovRisk)` = 8.00 + 6.66 + 11.34 + 9.60 + 9.12 + 5.04 = **49.76 ≈ 50/100**
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100** = 50 × (68/100) = **34/100**
- **Governance Rating:** Weak (40–54 band; also cannot exceed "Watchlist" per the RF-MGT-005 conviction cap — the natural Weak rating already sits below that ceiling, so the cap confirms rather than changes the result)
- **Confidence Score /100:** 68 — ownership, insider-transaction, and capital-allocation data are strong (confidence 4–5/5 throughout), but incentive alignment and roughly half of the board/shareholder-rights read rely on an unfiled proxy or web-sourced syntheses (confidence 2–3/5), and the single worst-quarter transcript (FQ2 FY26) is absent from the pool.
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** 9 / 2

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** Oracle is now funding its dividend and a large share of its AI-infrastructure capex with borrowed and freshly raised capital, not operating cash — FY2026 free cash flow was -$23.7bn against $5.8bn of dividends paid, cumulative uses of capital over FY2022–FY2026 ran to 233% of cumulative operating cash flow, and the resulting 53.7% one-year debt increase triggered an S&P downgrade to BBB- [`02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`, findings 02-001, 02-009, 02-012].
- **Strongest bull point:** Founder Lawrence Ellison holds 40.21% of the company (~$168.5bn), remains an active Executive Chair and CTO rather than a retired figurehead, and the demand signal behind the capital-spending ramp is real and large — remaining performance obligations grew 363% to $638bn in one year, not a promotional claim but a disclosed, audited backlog figure [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, finding 01-012; `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`, finding 04-001].
- **Single killer risk:** The unresolved securities class action alleging Oracle overstated the AI-deal growth outlook that the entire capital-spending program depends on (RF-MGT-005) — if that allegation is later substantiated, it would mean the demand backlog funding $115.8bn of new debt and equity was misrepresented to investors, which would invalidate both the capital-allocation bull case and the candor read simultaneously.
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** the CFO proactively volunteered the less-flattering ex-one-off EPS figure unprompted on the Q4 FY26 call ($6.83 vs. $7.63 headline) [`06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`, finding 06-002]; no pledging is disclosed and company policy bans it; there is no dual-class structure; and the company self-disclosed the $15bn capex overshoot the same cycle it became known rather than delaying it — genuine positive candor signals sit alongside the bear case, not absent from it.

## 2. Specialist Roll-Up

| Specialist | Verdict Line | Biggest Finding |
|---|---|---|
| governance-data-triage | Partial — ownership, insider, and capital-allocation data are strong; no 2026 proxy exists yet (due ~Sept 28, 2026) | 2026 Proxy Statement is the single highest-value missing document; would resolve both the incentive-alignment and board-and-shareholder-rights caps at once |
| management-and-track-record | Management Quality 57/100 — deep internal bench for CEO succession, but a genuine CFO-stability warning and a mixed promise-vs-delivery record | Two CFO-seat changes in 8 months landed in the same fiscal year as a 54% debt increase and a credit downgrade; unresolved securities class action (RF-MGT-005) caps Management Quality at 60 |
| capital-allocation-scorecard | Mixed, leaning value-destructive on the most recent two years against a genuinely value-creative earlier record | Uses of capital FY2022–FY2026 equal 233% of cumulative CFO; FY2026 dividend and much of capex are funded by $115.8bn of new debt/preferred/equity, not operations |
| incentives-and-compensation | Mixed — cannot be judged for either co-CEO (no proxy); the one disclosed metric (CFO's PSOs) is revenue, not per-share | Only 20% of the disclosed CFO equity grant carries any performance condition, and that condition is a size-based revenue target, not a returns or per-share metric |
| ownership-and-insider-behavior | Founder stake is a real alignment anchor, but trailing-12-month insider activity is unambiguous net selling | Every dollar-material Form 4 from a named executive in the trailing 12 months is a sale; zero open-market cash purchases by Ellison or either co-CEO on record at any point in the entire 2009–2026 history |
| board-and-shareholder-rights | Weak / entrenched — nominal structural protections exist but are materially weakened by founder/insider board concentration | 18–22% say-on-pay opposition at each of the last two annual meetings, alongside 5 of 13 board seats held by founder/former-CEO/current-CEO insiders and two independent directors with 29–32 years of tenure |
| candor-and-disclosure-quality | Mixed — specific, numbers-heavy disclosure where it counts, offset by recurring "one-off" adjustments and a promotional-vs-numbers gap | Prepared remarks describe a "tremendous" or "overdelivering" beat streak while the clean (ex one-off) operating beat rate over the trailing four quarters is 50%, roughly a coin flip |

## 3. Reconciliation

Two specialist disagreements are worth stating explicitly rather than averaging away.

**Ownership quality vs. recent insider behavior (04).** The ownership read is internally two-sided: Ellison's 40.21% stake is scored Green (20/25) as the strongest alignment signal in the profile, while the same agent scores trailing-12-month insider activity Red (6/20, RF-MKT-001) because every named executive sold stock and none bought. Both readings rest on the same evidentiary base and are not in tension — a large legacy stake built from option exercises is a different fact from what management is doing with its shares today. This synthesis treats the stake as a real but backward-looking alignment anchor and the selling pattern as the more decision-relevant, forward-looking signal, consistent with the module's own "judge actions, not words" principle; the more conservative reading (net selling, no current buying conviction) governs the Ownership Alignment component of shareholder friendliness.

**Board mechanics (05) vs. ownership control read (04).** Agent 04 concludes Oracle is "not a formally controlled company" (Ellison below the 50% threshold) and that Filter 6 (structurally unaligned owner) does not apply — a reasonable, narrow, technical read. Agent 05 separately concludes minority-shareholder protection is "weak / entrenched" because Ellison's 40.21% vote, combined with 5 of 13 board seats held by founder/current-CEO/former-CEO insiders and a plurality (not majority) director-election standard, gives a dissatisfied minority no reliable mechanism to remove an entrenched nominee. These are not contradictory findings — a company can be non-controlled in the formal NYSE sense while still functioning, in practice, as founder-influenced for any actual contested vote. This synthesis adopts the more conservative reading: Ellison is not a disqualifying structurally-unaligned owner (Filter 6 correctly does not trip), but board entrenchment risk is real and materially discounts the shareholder-friendliness score, consistent with agent 05's own numeric score (57/100, mixed band).

No other material disagreements between specialists were found; capital allocation (02), incentives (03), and candor (06) each independently corroborate the same underlying story — a company financing an unprecedented capital-spending cycle on debt and equity issuance rather than operating cash, with pay design and disclosure that have not yet been tested by a full compensation cycle or a genuinely bad quarter's verbatim transcript.

Both are propagated as standalone lines in the Red-Flag Register (Section 5A) for the master synthesizer's cross-module forensic roll-up alongside `earnings/06`'s RF-EQ series and `balance-sheet-survival/05`'s RF-OBS-001, per synthesizer.md Pre-Write Gate step 4B. RF-MGT-005 is likewise propagated as a standalone line below, not merely folded into the Management Quality score.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Oracle's leadership has partially delivered on what it has promised and partially not: the demand side of the AI-infrastructure bet is real and large (RPO +363% to $638bn), but the one specific, dated capital-spending promise management made — the FY2026 capex level implied by its own post-Q1 guidance — broke by roughly $15bn within a single quarter, and the market punished it with a 10.8% one-day stock decline. Capital has created real per-share value in the past (diluted shares fell 34.4% from FY2017 to the FY2023 trough) but has destroyed it over the trailing two years: buybacks have effectively stopped, share count is rising again, and the dividend plus a large share of capex are now funded by $115.8bn of new debt, preferred stock, and equity-plan proceeds rather than the business's own cash generation. Incentives cannot be judged for either co-CEO because no proxy exists yet, and the single disclosed metric — the new CFO's performance stock options — rewards revenue growth, not returns or per-share value, in the same year free cash flow turned sharply negative. Minority shareholders retain formal protections (single share class, annual elections, independent Audit and Compensation Committees) but sit inside a structure where the founder controls 40.21% of the vote, holds the Chair and CTO seats, and where 18–22% of shareholders voted against executive pay at each of the last two annual meetings without ever forcing a change. Management is candid about numbers when it discloses them — the CFO volunteered the less-flattering ex-one-off EPS figure unprompted, and the capex miss was quantified and disclosed the same cycle it emerged — but disclosure quality has a real gap this module could not close: the single worst quarter's verbatim transcript is missing from the record, and an unresolved securities class action alleging the AI-deal growth outlook itself was overstated has not been substantively engaged by management beyond standard legal boilerplate. The single most important reason to be cautious with this team's stewardship of capital right now is not any one red flag but their combination: an untested co-CEO structure, a CFO four months into the seat, zero disclosed pay-for-performance detail, and an unresolved integrity allegation, all arriving in the same year the company took on its largest-ever leverage increase to fund a bet that has not yet converted from backlog into cash.

## 5B. Peer Governance Benchmark

`business-model/08_competitive-map.md` names Microsoft (MSFT), Amazon (AMZN), and SAP as Oracle's closest scaled peers, but no specialist in this module pulled quantified peer governance metrics (board independence %, promoter/insider holding %, pledge %, non-audit/audit fee ratio, RPT intensity, or AGM votes-against) for any of the three — agent 05 explicitly flagged this as a gap ("No detailed peer governance data pulled" — finding 05-001). The only peer comparisons in the record are qualitative and web-sourced: agent 05 notes MSFT and SAP typically see single-digit-percent say-on-pay opposition versus Oracle's 18–22% (labeled Inference, not from filings), and that none of the three peers carries a comparable ~40% single-founder ownership bloc. A quantified peer table is not assessable from this module's data pool.

## 5D. Analyst Follow-Up Questions

- **RF-MGT-001 (CFO turnover):** Was Maxson's hire a genuine best-available-candidate search, or a hurried fill during a stability gap? Does her first two quarters of guidance accuracy (from FQ1 FY2027, due Sept 4, 2026) show a learning-curve miss or a clean start? [`01_management-and-track-record.md`]
- **RF-CAP-001 (capex guidance miss):** Was the $15bn overshoot a one-time forecasting error tied to the AI-buildout's unprecedented scale, or does it recur in FY2027 against the ~$70bn net-capex guide? Is the miss company-specific or an industry-wide AI-capex pattern (peers should be checked)? [`01_management-and-track-record.md`; `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`]
- **RF-CAP-002/003 (buybacks stopped; dividend/capex debt-funded):** Is management deliberately prioritizing the AI-infrastructure buildout over shareholder returns as a temporary, disclosed strategic choice, or is this an unstated policy drift investors should have been told about explicitly? Is the dividend at risk of a cut if FY2027 free cash flow does not recover, and would that be material to income-oriented holders? [`02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`]
- **RF-MGT-005 (unresolved integrity signal):** Is the underlying allegation — that the AI-deal growth outlook was overstated — supported by anything beyond the three cited factual predicates (an analyst note, the capex miss, and a timeline-delay report), all of which are independently disclosed and none of which is itself an admission? Does the case survive a motion to dismiss? Is this company-specific or part of a broader wave of AI-infrastructure-related securities litigation across the sector? Does it affect minority holders differently from the controlling founder? [`01_management-and-track-record.md`; `06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`]
- **RF-MKT-001 (net insider selling):** Are the sale clusters fully explained by scheduled Rule 10b5-1 plans (the pool infers but does not confirm this), and do sales continue or pause around the FQ1 FY2027 print? [`04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`]
- **RF-SHR-001 (say-on-pay opposition):** Is the 18–22% opposition driven by pay magnitude, pay-metric design (size- vs. per-share-based), or both? Does ISS/Glass Lewis published rationale exist for the 2026 vote once the proxy season begins? [`05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`]
- **RF-DISC-001/002 (promotional framing; recurring restructuring one-offs):** Is the gap between prepared-remarks tone and the clean beat rate a company-specific pattern or standard large-cap earnings-call practice? Does a third consecutive restructuring plan appear in FY2027, and if so, should it be treated as a normal operating expense rather than a one-off? [`06_candor-and-disclosure-quality.md`; `earnings/06_earnings-quality.md`]

## 6. What Would Change The Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Misaligned or weak stewardship | The 2026 Proxy Statement discloses per-share or returns-based (ROIC/EPS/FCF-per-share/TSR) metrics for both co-CEOs with meaningful weight; the securities class action is dismissed or settled without a finding of wrongdoing; FY2027 free cash flow recovers to cover the dividend without further debt issuance; buybacks resume at a scale that offsets SBC dilution; say-on-pay opposition falls toward single digits at the 2026 AGM | The class action survives a motion to dismiss or is amended with stronger factual allegations; a further CFO or senior-executive departure occurs within FY2027; FY2027 capex again overshoots guidance by a material margin; the dividend is cut or debt is raised again to fund it; insider selling accelerates ahead of a weak print | 2026 Proxy Statement (DEF 14A, due ~Sept 28, 2026); FQ1 FY2027 results (due Sept 4, 2026) and the FQ2 FY2026 (Dec 10, 2025) verbatim transcript to close the candor gap; the motion-to-dismiss ruling on the securities class action |

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- **Stewardship verdict:** Misaligned or weak stewardship. The strongest single piece of evidence is the capital-structure sequencing: $115.8bn of new debt, preferred stock, and equity-plan proceeds funded uses of capital that exceeded operating cash flow by 233% over FY2022–FY2026, culminating in a 53.7% one-year debt increase and an S&P downgrade to BBB-.
- **Capital-allocation record:** Genuinely value-creative from FY2017–FY2023 (diluted shares −34.4% via buybacks) but value-destructive over the trailing two years — buybacks are now a token gesture (1.9% of stock-based compensation expense in FY2026), share count is rising again (+5.3% off the FY2023 trough), and the dividend is not covered by free cash flow (FY2026 coverage: −409.4%).
- **Incentive/ownership alignment:** Cannot be assessed for either co-CEO — no proxy exists yet. The one disclosed metric (the new CFO's performance stock options, 20% of her equity grant) rewards revenue growth, a size-based metric, not per-share value. Founder Ellison's 40.21% stake is a real alignment anchor built through option exercise rather than open-market purchase; every named executive sold stock in the trailing 12 months with zero offsetting buys.
- **Biggest governance risk / red flag:** RF-MGT-005 — an unresolved securities class action (filed 2026-02-03) alleging management overstated the AI-infrastructure deal growth outlook that the entire capital-spending program depends on. Critical materiality, not cleared, not proven. Caps management quality and disclosure candor at 60 and caps the governance rating at no better than "Watchlist" until resolved. A second Critical-materiality item, RF-CAP-003 (dividend/uses of capital not covered by free cash flow), independently reinforces the same ceiling.
- **Hard disqualifier:** None. `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md` verbatim: "No disqualifier triggered... Action: No verdict lock applies from this scan." The scan separately routes the securities class action as a soft signal (see above), not a lock.
- **§24 rejector filters:** Filter 1 (integrity) — tripped, RF-MGT-005, caps management quality/candor at 60 and conviction at "Watchlist." Filter 2 (turnaround) — did not trip; Oracle is a healthy, growing incumbent, not a turnaround. Filter 4 (serial acquirer) — did not trip; only two material deals in ten years, six years apart. Filter 6 (unaligned owner) — did not trip; Ellison is the founder with concentrated wealth in the business, the opposite of the filter's target profile.
- **Partial-data caps applied:** Incentive alignment and board/shareholder-rights reads are both limited by the absence of the 2026 Proxy Statement (due ~Sept 28, 2026); disclosure candor is limited by the absence of the FQ2 FY2026 (worst-quarter) verbatim transcript — none of these caps bound the natural (already-lower) scores except disclosure candor, where the RF-MGT-005 conviction cap reduced the natural score from 65 to 60.
- **Biggest missing data point:** The 2026 Proxy Statement (DEF 14A) — due on or before ~Sept 28, 2026. It would resolve the incentive-alignment cap (CEO compensation structure and metrics), the board-disclosure cap (full committee composition, formal independence determinations), and the related-party-transaction detail (Item 13) in one document. This is a genuine future filing, not currently obtainable data — recommend re-running this module once it is filed.
- **Explicit handoff:** This module supersedes the `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` quick-read. The master synthesizer should treat this module's governance verdict and scores as the primary governance read.

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management has broken at least one specific, dated promise this year — it missed its own capex guidance by about $15 billion within a single quarter, and the stock fell 10.8% the day it admitted it.
- Capital allocation created real value for shareholders through 2023 (buybacks cut the share count by a third) but has destroyed value since — the company is now paying its dividend and funding much of its AI-data-center spending with borrowed money, not the cash the business generates.
- The pay plan cannot be judged yet because the pay-disclosure document (the 2026 proxy) has not been filed; the one number that is disclosed — a new CFO's bonus stock tied to revenue growth — rewards getting bigger, not making more money per share.
- Insiders have real skin in the game (the founder owns 40% of the company, worth about $168 billion) but built it almost entirely through stock-option exercises, not cash purchases, and every top executive sold stock in the past year with none buying.
- Minority shareholders have some real protections (one share, one vote, annual board elections) but the founder holds outsized board and voting influence, and 18–22% of shareholders have voted against the pay plan at each of the last two annual meetings without forcing a change.
- Management is candid about numbers when it reports them — a less-flattering profit figure was volunteered unprompted on the last earnings call — but an unresolved lawsuit alleging the company overstated its AI-deal growth outlook has not been substantively addressed beyond standard legal boilerplate.
- No hard governance disqualifier (fraud, going concern, pledging, related-party abuse) was found.
- This module is useful for the next reader with one caveat: several of its scores are provisional and should be revisited once the 2026 pay-and-board disclosure document is filed, expected by late September 2026.

# Management & Track Record — ORCL

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Co-CEO | Clayton M. Magouyrk (39) | Sept 22, 2025 | ~11 months as co-CEO; joined Oracle 2014 (~11.5 yrs at company) | Ran Oracle Cloud Infrastructure engineering and cloud infrastructure as EVP (Dec 2019–Jun 2025) before becoming President, OCI, then co-CEO; prior to Oracle, senior engineer at Amazon/AWS (2008–2014) — direct hyperscale-cloud operating background matches Oracle's current strategic bet | [FY26 10-K, Item 10 (Executive Officers of the Registrant)] |
| Co-CEO | Michael D. Sicilia (55) | Sept 22, 2025 | ~11 months as co-CEO; joined Oracle 2009 (~16.5 yrs at company) | Ran Oracle's Industries and Global Business Units as EVP (Oct 2019–Jun 2025) before becoming President, Industries, then co-CEO; prior to Oracle, CTO at Primavera Systems (1993–2008) | [FY26 10-K, Item 10] |
| CFO | Hilary Maxson (48) | Apr 6, 2026 | ~4 months in seat as of this filing | External hire; Group CFO of Schneider Electric SE (2020–2026), prior CFO roles within Schneider's Energy Management and Building/IT units, 12 years earlier at AES Corp in finance/strategy/M&A on capital-intensive infrastructure — background matches Oracle's current capex-heavy build-out, but no Oracle-specific track record yet | [FY26 10-K, Item 10]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, "Oracle Corporation Announces CFO Changes, Effective April 6, 2026"] |
| Bridge CFO / Principal Financial Officer | Douglas A. Kehring (52) | Sept 22, 2025 – Apr 6, 2026 (interim) | ~26 years at Oracle (joined 2000) | Long-tenured internal executive (EVP Operations since 2015, prior SVP Corporate Development & Strategic Planning); bridged the CFO seat for ~7 months between Catz's departure and Maxson's arrival, then reverted to EVP, Operations — continuity hire, not a crisis-driven external emergency appointment | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, "Oracle Corporation Promotes Doug Kehring to Principal Financial Officer, Effective September 22, 2025" and "...CFO Changes, Effective April 6, 2026"] |
| Executive Chair & CTO / Co-Founder | Lawrence J. Ellison (81) | Executive Chair & CTO since Sept 2014; founded the company June 1977 | Director since 1977 (49 years); CEO 1977–2014 (37 years) | Founder; still holds the Chief Technology Officer title and ~40.2% of common stock — an active, engaged controlling founder-shareholder rather than a retired figurehead | [FY26 10-K, Item 10]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Top Holders table] |
| Former CEO / Exec Vice Chair | Safra A. Catz (64) | CEO Sept 2014–Sept 2025 (11 years); Exec Vice Chair since Sept 2025 | Director since 2001 (25 years); at Oracle since 1999 | Longest-serving CEO in the review period; moved to a Board role rather than leaving the company entirely, and also sits on outside boards (Paramount Skydance) | [FY26 10-K, Item 10] |

**Founder/owner-operator status:** Oracle is founder-influenced but not founder-run. Larry Ellison founded the company and remains Executive Chair, CTO and its largest shareholder at ~40.2% of common stock [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Top Holders table, "Ellison, Lawrence J., 1,158,232,353 shares, 40.21%"], but he stepped down as CEO in 2014 and the operating CEO role now sits with two non-founder, internal-promotion co-CEOs. This is a hybrid: a founder-controlled board with professional-manager day-to-day leadership, not a classic owner-operator CEO structure.

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures (last 3 years) | CEO Safra Catz stepped down after 11 years (Sept 2025) into a Board role (not an exit from the company); no CFO, General Counsel, or Chief Accounting Officer departure in the period beyond the CFO seat transition below. Two long-tenured independent directors (George Conrades, 18 years; Naomi Seligman, 20 years) retired in Jan 2026, replaced by two new independent directors (Rusckowski, Nov 2025; Mihaljevic, May 2026) — framed as ordinary board refreshment, not resignations citing concerns | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, "Executive and Board Changes, Effective September 22, 2025"; "Retirement of Directors George H. Conrades and Naomi O. Seligman," 2026-01-09; "Names Stephen Rusckowski to the Board," 2025-11-21; "Elects Dr. Tomislav Mihaljevic to Board," 2026-05-12] |
| CFO turnover | **Two CFO-seat changes inside an 8-month window**: Catz (CFO through 2014, by then long removed from the role) → interim Principal Financial Officer Doug Kehring (Sept 22, 2025) → external hire Hilary Maxson (Apr 6, 2026). This lands in the same fiscal year Oracle raised total debt 54% (to $167.4bn) and was downgraded by S&P to BBB- (Jul 9, 2026) [`business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`] — a CFO seat in flux during the company's largest-ever leverage and capex ramp is a genuine stability warning, even though each individual step (internal bridge, then an experienced external hire) was orderly rather than a sudden resignation | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, CFO-change entries above] |
| CEO succession / continuity | Oracle moved from a single, 11-year CEO (Catz) to an **unprecedented co-CEO structure** (Magouyrk/Sicilia) — both internal promotions with 11+ and 16+ years at the company respectively, not outside hires, which reduces (but does not eliminate) execution risk from the transition. The 10-K states there were "no arrangements or understandings between Mr. Magouyrk and any other persons pursuant to which he was appointed" — standard SEC succession language, not independently verified evidence of a smooth transition | [FY26 10-K, Item 10]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, 2025-09-22 entries] |

**Read:** the CEO transition itself is lower-risk than it looks — both new co-CEOs are long-tenured internal operators, and the bridge CFO (Kehring, 26 years at Oracle) prevented a leadership vacuum. The CFO seat is the more genuine warning: two changes in 8 months, with the incoming CFO having zero Oracle-specific track record as of this filing, landing exactly when the balance sheet risk is highest (debt +54% in one year, credit downgrade already realized). This is flagged, not glossed over.

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management says Oracle is pivoting from an enterprise-software/database incumbent into a large-scale AI cloud-infrastructure (OCI) supplier, funded by a $638 billion remaining performance obligation (RPO) backlog that grew 363% in one year [FY26 10-K, Item 7 MD&A; Q4 FY26 earnings call, CFO Maxson]. The explicit, checkable targets set on the Jun 10, 2026 (Q4 FY26) call and press release are: FY2027 total revenue of $90 billion (point guidance), FY2027 non-GAAP EPS of $8.05 (raised from a prior figure, ~18% growth once one-time gains are excluded from the base), FY2027 net cash capex of roughly $70 billion (implying gross capex in the $90–95 billion area once customer prepayments are added back), and a capital-financing plan to raise about $40 billion through debt and equity (including the already-announced $20 billion at-the-market equity program), with no additional debt issuance planned in calendar 2026 [Oracle_Earnings Press Release Q4FY26.pdf, "Guidance for Full FY 2027"; Q4 FY26 earnings call, CFO prepared remarks].

## 4. Promise vs Delivery

| Prior Promise / Target | Period Set | Actual Outcome | Kept / Missed | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FY2026 capital expenditure level implied by guidance issued after Q1 FY26 (~Sept 2025) | Set ~Sept 2025 (post-Q1 FY26 call) | On the Dec 10, 2025 (Q2 FY26) call, Oracle disclosed it "now expect[ed] fiscal 2026 CapEx will be about $15 billion higher than [it] forecasted after Q1," alongside a revenue-growth and free-cash-flow miss versus Street consensus that quarter. Stock fell $24.16 (−10.8%) the next day | **Missed** — capex ran materially above what was signaled just one quarter earlier | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, 2026-02-05 entry quoting the Q2 FY26 print and stock reaction] |
| FQ1 FY2026 revenue and EPS (Street-consensus-referenced, no separate Oracle point guidance found for the specific quarter in the pool) | Guided ~Jun 2025 | Actual revenue $14,926M vs estimate $15,043M (−0.78%); actual non-GAAP EPS $1.47 vs estimate $1.479 (−0.68%) | **Missed** (narrowly, on both lines) | [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6, citing Capital IQ Estimates Report Surprise tab] |
| FQ2 FY2026 revenue and EPS | Guided ~Sept 2025 | Revenue missed by −0.83%; non-GAAP EPS beat by +37.8%, but the beat was driven by one-time investment gains (Ampere/Bloom Energy), not operating performance — GAAP EPS beat by +79.5% the same quarter | **Mixed / not a clean operating beat** — the headline EPS "beat" overstates underlying delivery | [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6] |
| FQ3 FY2026 revenue and EPS | Guided ~Dec 2025 | Revenue beat +1.66%; EPS beat +5.9%, no footnoted one-off | **Kept / clean beat** | [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6] |
| FQ4 FY2026 revenue and EPS | Guided ~Mar 2026 | Revenue beat +0.46%; EPS beat +7.65% headline, but $2.7bn Ampere-sale gain and Bloom Energy warrant gains inflated the figure — ex-gains non-GAAP EPS was $2.03 (vs. $1.962 estimate), a genuine but smaller beat of +3.4% | **Kept, but smaller than headline once one-offs are stripped** | [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6; FY26 10-K, Item 7 MD&A "Non-Operating Income (Expense), Net"] |
| FY2027 revenue $90bn / non-GAAP EPS $8.05 (current guidance) | Set Jun 10, 2026 | Not yet reportable — first FY2027 print is FQ1 FY2027, due Sept 4, 2026, after this run's date | **Not yet assessable** — flagged for the next earnings cycle | [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 2–3] |

**Net read:** across the four most recent reported quarters, Oracle beat on revenue in 2 of 4 and beat on EPS in 3 of 4 as reported, but once the one-time Ampere/Bloom Energy gains are stripped from two of those EPS "beats," the clean operating beat rate falls to roughly 2 of 4 — closer to a coin flip than a track record of reliably beating its own numbers [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6]. The one specific, dated promise that clearly broke was the FY2026 capex level: management under-forecast its own capital spending by about $15 billion within a single quarter, and the market punished it (−10.8% same-week reaction). Set against that, current FY2027 guidance sits within roughly ±0.1%–0.7% of Street consensus on revenue and EPS [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 3] — management is not currently sandbagging or over-promising versus what analysts already expect, which is a different (and better) signal than the FY2026 capex miss, but it also means the FY2027 numbers have not yet been tested against actual delivery.

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | **No.** Oracle is not a struggling business being rescued — FY2026 revenue grew 17% to $67.4bn, cloud infrastructure revenue grew 77%, and RPO grew 363% to $638bn [`business-model/02_business-identity.md`]. The new co-CEOs and new CFO arrived at a financially healthy, fast-growing incumbent executing an existing strategic pivot (AI cloud infrastructure), not a distressed company needing a rescue plan. Filter 2's turnaround base-rate penalty does not apply | [FY26 10-K, Item 7 MD&A; Q4FY26 Earnings Press Release] |
| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | Not applicable (no turnaround thesis) | — |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | Partially relevant even without a turnaround framing: the new co-CEOs' and CFO's résumés (AWS engineering background, Schneider Electric capital-intensive-infrastructure CFO experience) are being implicitly cited by the company and by sell-side commentary as reasons the leadership team can execute the AI-infrastructure build-out. This report treats those résumés as background, not as evidence of delivery — the delivery test is the promise-vs-delivery table in Section 4, not the biographies in Section 1 | [FY26 10-K, Item 10] |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (unverified adverse signal) | **Yes — chased below.** A private securities class action (filed Feb 3, 2026, D. Delaware) alleges Oracle and its executives made misleading statements overstating the AI-deal growth outlook between Jun 12, 2025 and Dec 16, 2025. The specific factual predicates cited by plaintiffs' counsel: (1) a Sept 25, 2025 sell-side note (Rothschild & Co Redburn) argued the market was "materially overestimat[ing]" AI-deal-driven growth (stock −5.6% that day); (2) the Dec 10, 2025 Q2 FY26 print revealed the ~$15bn capex-guidance miss described in Section 4 (stock −10.8%); (3) a Dec 12, 2025 Bloomberg report that Oracle pushed back completion dates for OpenAI data centers from 2027 to 2028 citing labor and material shortages. As of the FY26 10-K date, the case was still pre-motion-to-dismiss — unresolved, not proven, not dismissed | [`business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, Section 3]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key Developments.xls, 2026-02-05 entry, "Law Offices of Howard G. Smith Announces Filing of Class Action Lawsuit"]; [FY26 10-K, Note 15, "Legal Proceedings — Securities Class Action Regarding Oracle Cloud Infrastructure"] |

**Turnaround rule application:** Not applicable — see row 1. Oracle is classified as an established, growing incumbent undergoing a capital-intensive strategic pivot with new leadership, not a governance-turnaround thesis.

**Integrity rule application:** This report chased the routed signal as far as the data pool allows. The pool contains: the plaintiffs' factual narrative (a law-firm press release, not a court filing or company admission), the underlying disclosed facts it is built on (the capex-guidance revision, the analyst note, the Bloomberg report — all independently corroborated in Key Developments and the 10-K), and the company's own framing (RPO growth, customer-concentration disclosure, and its explicit backing-out of one-time gains from FY2027 guidance). None of the pool's sources — not the 10-K, not the two earnings-call transcripts, not the Key Developments log — contains a motion-to-dismiss ruling, a settlement, an SEC finding, or a company admission that would clear or escalate this allegation. **The signal remains unresolved**, neither cleared by primary evidence nor escalated to a proven fact. Per the module rule, this is emitted as a standalone red flag below.

**RF-MGT-005 (unresolved adverse integrity signal, unproven)** — Securities class action alleging Oracle overstated its AI-infrastructure deal growth outlook (filed Feb 3, 2026, U.S. District Court, District of Delaware; pre-motion-to-dismiss as of the FY26 10-K date). Not cleared by primary evidence; not proven. Caps Management Quality at 60 and conviction at no better than "Watchlist" until resolved, per MODULE_RULES.md Score Cap Rules.

## 5. Management Read

Oracle's leadership bench is deep and internally seasoned — both new co-CEOs are long-tenured Oracle veterans (11 and 16+ years) rather than outside hires, and the 26-year-veteran who bridged the CFO seat prevented a leadership vacuum during the transition — but the CFO chair itself changed hands twice in eight months, landing in the exact fiscal year Oracle's debt rose 54% to $167.4bn and its credit rating was cut to BBB-, which is a genuine timing-and-stability concern, not a cosmetic one. The promise-vs-delivery record is mixed, not clean: the clear, dated miss is the ~$15 billion FY2026 capex-guidance overshoot that management itself disclosed and that cost the stock 10.8% in a day, while the quarterly beat streak that looks strong on the surface (beats in 3 of the last 4 EPS prints) shrinks to roughly a coin flip once one-time Ampere/Bloom Energy gains are stripped out of two of those beats. The single biggest management signal is the unresolved securities class action alleging the AI-deal growth outlook itself was overstated — an allegation this report could not clear or dismiss from the available filings, so it is carried forward as a live, capped risk (RF-MGT-005) rather than discounted because the company's audited financials are otherwise clean.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — ORCL

Reporting currency: USD. Fiscal year ends May 31 (Oracle "FY2026" = year ended May-31-2026). All figures from audited filings or Capital IQ exports as cited; a discrepancy between Capital IQ's cash-flow-statement buyback figures and the 10-K's own disclosed buyback figures for FY2025/FY2026 is flagged and resolved in favor of the filing per CLAUDE.md §5.

## 1. Uses of Capital (5 years, FY2022–FY2026)

| Use | Cumulative Amount | Currency | % of CFO | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| Acquisitions (net cash) | 27,869 | USD mm | 28.4% | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Cash Acquisitions" row, FY2022–FY2026] |
| Buybacks | 23,779 | USD mm | 24.2% | FY2022–FY2024 from [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Repurchase of Common Stock"]; FY2025–FY2026 from [FY26 10-K, "Common Stock Repurchase Program," "We repurchased 0.4 million shares for $93 million and 3.9 million shares for $600 million in fiscal 2026 and 2025, respectively"] — the filing's own figures are used for FY25–FY26 in preference to Capital IQ's cash-flow-tab figures of $1,500mm (FY25) and $206mm (FY26), which appear to include a broader repurchase definition (e.g., shares withheld for employee tax on RSU vesting) than the Board-authorized buyback program the 10-K reports on |
| Dividends (common + preferred) | 22,046 | USD mm | 22.5% | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Total Dividends Paid," FY2022–FY2026]; [FY26 10-K, "In fiscal 2026, we declared and paid aggregate cash dividends of $1,263.89 per share of outstanding Mandatory Convertible Preferred Stock that totaled $63 million and $2.00 per share of outstanding common stock that totaled $5.7 billion"] |
| Capex | 96,950 | USD mm | 98.8% | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Capital Expenditure," FY2022–FY2026] |
| Debt repayment (scheduled + commercial paper) | 58,035 | USD mm | 59.1% | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Repaid," FY2022–FY2026] |
| **Memo: cumulative CFO, FY2022–FY2026** | 98,175 | USD mm | 100% | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Cash from Ops." row] |
| **Memo: cumulative debt + preferred + common-stock issuance, FY2022–FY2026** | 115,763 | USD mm | 117.9% of CFO | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Total Debt Issued" ($106,291mm) + "Issuance of Pref. Stock" ($4,954mm) + "Issuance of Common Stock" ($4,518mm)] |

Total uses of capital over the period ($228,679mm) exceed cumulative cash from operations ($98,175mm) by roughly $130,504mm. That gap was not closed by cutting capex or buybacks — it was closed by raising $115,763mm of new debt, preferred stock and common-stock-plan proceeds over the same five years, concentrated almost entirely in FY2025–FY2026. This is the single structural fact behind every row below: Oracle is now funding its dividend, and a large share of its capex, with borrowed and freshly-raised capital, not with cash the business is throwing off.

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|---|
| NetSuite, Inc. | Closed Nov-2016 (FY2017) | $9.3bn cash (Oracle's FY2017 total cash acquisitions of $11,221mm include this deal plus smaller add-ons) | Add a cloud ERP suite for small/mid-market customers Oracle's own on-premise stack did not serve | Not separately assessable — Oracle discloses no standalone NetSuite revenue or return figure; NetSuite is folded into the "Cloud and Software" segment's SaaS applications line with no sub-line breakout in the audited segment note [FY26 10-K, Note 13]. No goodwill impairment has ever been recorded against this deal | Low — the deal was cash-funded at a point when Oracle's capex was modest (~$2.0bn FY2017) and did not force any disclosed divestiture or strategic pivot; limited opportunity cost | [FY26 10-K, Note 13 (Segment Information)]; [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, FY2017 "Cash Acquisitions" row] |
| Cerner Corporation (now "Oracle Health") | Closed Jun-2022 (FY2023) | $28.3bn total deal value; $27,721mm cash paid per the cash-flow statement | Enter healthcare IT / electronic health records, and cross-sell Oracle Cloud Infrastructure (OCI) into hospital systems | Not separately assessable — Oracle Health is likewise folded into "Cloud and Software" with no standalone revenue, margin, or synergy-realization disclosure in any filing in the pool. Product-level news (AI patient portal, AI clinical-note agent, Theator surgical-AI partnership) shows continued product investment, not a disclosed financial return. No goodwill impairment has been recorded ("Our most recent goodwill impairment analysis was performed on March 1, 2026 and did not result in a goodwill impairment charge") | Real — the deal was debt-funded: total debt rose from $75,859mm (FY2022) to $90,481mm (FY2023), a $14.6bn increase in the deal year [Financials_Annual.xls, Capital-Structure-Summary tab]. That debt capacity was drawn down three years before Oracle needed to raise $167.4bn of total debt (FY2026) to fund the AI-infrastructure buildout — the Cerner deal consumed leverage headroom that, with hindsight, would have been more valuable held in reserve for the OCI capex cycle that followed. This is the opportunity-cost charge against the deal, separate from and in addition to its own unproven return | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, FY2023 "Cash Acquisitions" row; Capital-Structure-Summary tab, "Total Debt" row]; [FY26 10-K, Note (Goodwill), "Our most recent goodwill impairment analysis..."]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Key-Developments.xls, Cerner/Oracle Health product-announcement entries, 2026] |

**Serial-acquirer test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4):** Not triggered. Oracle made two material acquisitions in a ten-year window (NetSuite FY2017, Cerner FY2023), spaced roughly six years apart, with zero cash acquisitions recorded in FY2024, FY2025 or FY2026 [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab]. This is periodic, large-deal M&A, not a continuous serial-acquirer pattern; RF-CAP-004 does not apply, consistent with `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md`'s own conclusion. That said, neither deal carries a disclosed, checkable return metric — "no goodwill impairment" is a floor (the deal has not obviously failed), not evidence the deal cleared its cost of capital. Absent a disclosed synergy target vs. actual, both deals are scored on the opportunity-cost test only, and Cerner fails that test materially given the debt-capacity consumption described above.

## 3. Buyback Scorecard

| Year | Amount Spent | Currency | Shares Bought | Implied Avg Price | Share-Count Change (diluted) | Offset SBC Dilution? | Price-Discipline Evidence | Judgment | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| FY2022 | 17,341 | USD mm | Not separately disclosed for this year in the pool | Not determinable from available data | 3,022mm → 2,786mm (−7.8%) | Yes — buyback $ (17,341) vastly exceeded SBC expense ($2,613mm) | Stock traded $67.04–$103.65 during FY2022 (Jun-2021–May-2022); average price not disclosed, so buy-low/buy-high cannot be judged | Buybacks materially reduced share count; the largest of the five years shown | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab; Income Statement tab, "Weighted Avg. Diluted Shares Out."; ORCL_Charting-Excel-Export, Pane 1] |
| FY2023 | 2,503 | USD mm | Not separately disclosed | Not determinable | 2,786mm → 2,766mm (−0.7%) | Marginally — buyback $ (2,503) was below SBC expense ($3,547mm), but the actual number of new shares issued from vesting is smaller than the SBC dollar figure, so share count still fell slightly | Buyback spend cut by 86% year-on-year in the same fiscal year the $27.7bn Cerner deal closed — cash was redirected to the acquisition, not returned to shareholders. Stock traded $61.07–$105.15 during FY2023 | Buybacks slowed sharply and barely net-reduced the share count; the Cerner deal, not price discipline, explains the pullback | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab; Income Statement tab] |
| FY2024 | 3,242 | USD mm | Not separately disclosed | Not determinable | 2,766mm → 2,823mm (+2.1%) | No — buyback $ (3,242) was below SBC expense ($3,974mm) and diluted share count rose | Stock traded $100.31–$129.24 during FY2024 | Buybacks failed to offset dilution for the first time in the five-year window; share count reversed higher | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab; Income Statement tab] |
| FY2025 | 600 (10-K); 1,500 (CIQ cash-flow tab, broader definition) | USD mm | 3.9 million | $153.85/share (=$600mm ÷ 3.9mm shares, computed from the 10-K's own disclosed dollars and share count) | 2,823mm → 2,866mm (+1.5%) | No — buyback $ (600) was 12.8% of SBC expense ($4,674mm) | Stock traded $119.28–$192.43 during FY2025; the $153.85 implied average sits roughly 47% up the range — a mid-range price, not evidence of buying at a trough | Buybacks reduced to a token amount and no longer offset dilution; share count rose | [FY26 10-K, "Common Stock Repurchase Program"]; [ORCL_Charting-Excel-Export, Pane 1] |
| FY2026 | 93 (10-K); 206 (CIQ, broader definition) | USD mm | 0.4 million | $232.50/share (=$93mm ÷ 0.4mm shares) | 2,866mm → 2,914mm (+1.7%) | No — buyback $ (93) was 1.9% of SBC expense ($4,811mm) | Stock traded $136.48–$328.33 during FY2026; the $232.50 implied average sits roughly at the midpoint of the range | Buybacks are now immaterial (0.3% of cumulative FY22–26 buyback spend) at the same time capex and debt exploded; the $6.3bn of remaining authorization as of May-31-2026 is unused, and the company states "no stock repurchase activity for the three months ended May 31, 2026" | [FY26 10-K, "Common Stock Repurchase Program," "As of May 31, 2026, approximately $6.3 billion remained available... There was no stock repurchase activity for the three months ended May 31, 2026"] |

This module runs before the valuation module in the pipeline, so buyback price discipline is judged only against the stock's own trading range within each fiscal year — not against an intrinsic/fair-value estimate. Intrinsic-value discipline is not assessable from this module; if the valuation module's fair-value range becomes available, the FY2025–FY2026 implied prices ($153.85 and $232.50) should be re-tested against it.

**Net read:** buybacks did the heavy lifting on per-share value from FY2017 through the FY2023 trough — diluted shares fell from 4,217mm (FY2017) to 2,766mm (FY2023), a 34.4% reduction [Financials_Annual.xls, Income Statement tab] — but that program has been effectively switched off since FY2024. Diluted share count has now risen for three straight years (2,766mm → 2,914mm, +5.3% off the FY2023 low), buybacks have fallen 99.5% from the FY2022 peak in dollar terms, and stock-based compensation ($4,811mm FY2026) plus a new $5.0bn Mandatory Convertible Preferred Stock (convertible into common by Jan-15-2029) point to further dilution ahead with no offsetting repurchase activity. **RF-CAP-002 (buybacks not reducing share count) is triggered for FY2024–FY2026.**

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---:|---|
| Payout ratio (common dividends / net income), FY2026 | 33.4% ($5,700mm common dividends ÷ $17,087mm net income) | [FY26 10-K, "declared and paid aggregate cash dividends of... $2.00 per share of outstanding common stock that totaled $5.7 billion"]; [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Net Income"] |
| Payout ratio, FY2022 (5-year comparison) | 51.5% ($3,457mm ÷ $6,717mm) | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab] |
| Payout-ratio trend, FY2022→FY2026 | 51.5% → 43.1% → 41.9% → 38.1% → 33.4%, steadily declining | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab] — declining payout ratio reflects net income growing faster than the dividend, not dividend restraint in response to cash-flow stress |
| Dividend coverage (FCF), FY2026 | Not covered — FCF of −$23,686mm against $5,787mm of total dividends paid (common + preferred) | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, "Levered Free Cash Flow" and "Total Dividends Paid" rows] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF), FY2025 | Not covered — FCF of −$394mm against $4,743mm dividends paid | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF), FY2022–FY2024 | Covered — 1.45x (FY2022), 2.31x (FY2023), 2.69x (FY2024) | [Financials_Annual.xls, Cash Flow tab, computed as FCF ÷ Total Dividends Paid] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital), FY2023→FY2026 (3-yr) | 7.0% (ΔNOPAT $6,823mm ÷ ΔInvested Capital $96,927mm) | Own computation: NOPAT = EBIT × (1 − effective tax rate) using [Financials_Annual.xls, Income Statement tab, "EBIT" and "Effective Tax Rate %" rows]; Invested Capital = Total Debt + Total Equity − Cash & Equivalents using [Financials_Annual.xls, Balance Sheet tab] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital), FY2021→FY2026 (5-yr) | 5.3% (ΔNOPAT $6,253mm ÷ ΔInvested Capital $119,100mm) | Same method; FY2021 effective tax rate was reported "NM" in the source data, so a 15% approximation was used for that single year's NOPAT — labeled: Inference, not from filings. This affects only the FY2021 base-year figure, not the FY2023–FY2026 leg of the computation |

The dividend itself is not large relative to earnings (33.4% payout, and falling) — the problem is not the dividend's size, it is that operations no longer generate enough cash to pay it. Both FY2025 and FY2026 dividends were effectively funded from the $115.8bn of debt, preferred stock, and common-stock-plan proceeds raised over the period (Section 1), not from free cash flow. **RF-CAP-003 (dividends not covered by FCF) is triggered for FY2025–FY2026**, consistent with the flag already raised in `business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance.md` and `01_management-and-track-record.md` (finding 01-013).

Incremental ROIC of 5.3%–7.0% describes the return the last several years of net new capital have earned so far. That return is being generated while the newest and largest tranche of capital — the FY2026 capex ramp ($55.7bn, more than 2.6x FY2025's level) — has barely begun to be reflected in revenue (RPO of $638bn is contracted-but-not-yet-recognized backlog, not realized revenue). This module does not compute or compare against a cost-of-capital figure (that is the valuation module's job), but flags that the incremental-ROIC figure so far is thin relative to the scale of capital being deployed, and its trajectory over the next 2–3 reporting periods is the single most important number for testing whether this capital allocation cycle creates or destroys per-share value.

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

Capital allocation at Oracle has been mixed, tilting value-destructive on a per-share basis over the most recent two years, after a genuinely value-creative stretch from FY2017–FY2023. The best use of capital in the period was the historical buyback program, which cut diluted shares by 34.4% from FY2017 to the FY2023 trough — real, verifiable per-share value creation — but that program is now effectively dead (FY2026 spend of $93mm is 0.5% of the FY2022 peak), and diluted share count has risen every year since, undoing part of that gain while stock-based compensation and a convertible-preferred overhang keep adding new shares. The worst use of capital is the dividend, not because of its size but because of what funds it: Oracle paid $5.8bn of dividends in FY2026 while free cash flow was −$23.7bn, meaning the payout — and a meaningful share of the AI-infrastructure capex build — is now financed by the $115.8bn of debt, preferred stock and equity-plan proceeds raised over the period, not by the business's own cash generation. The single biggest capital-allocation signal is the sequencing: the debt-funded Cerner acquisition (FY2023) consumed leverage capacity three years before management needed that same capacity for a far larger, AI-driven capex cycle, forcing FY2026's 54% one-year debt increase and the subsequent S&P downgrade to BBB-. Read together with a computed incremental ROIC of only 5.3%–7.0% on the last several years' net new capital, this is not evidence of disciplined per-share compounding — it is a business betting its capital structure on demand (the $638bn RPO backlog) converting to cash on a schedule that has not yet been tested. Verdict: **mixed**, leaning value-destructive on the most recent two years' allocation choices, against a genuinely value-creative earlier record.

# Incentives & Compensation — ORCL

## 1. Compensation Structure

Reporting currency: USD. Period covered: fiscal year 2026 (ended May 31, 2026) and the go-forward target-pay level set in the CFO's January 19, 2026 offer letter. **No 2026 Proxy Statement (DEF 14A) exists yet** — Oracle's FY26 10-K states Items 10–14 (director/executive compensation, ownership, related-party) are "incorporated by reference" from the 2026 Proxy Statement, due within 120 days of the May 31, 2026 fiscal year-end (~Sept 28, 2026) — after this run's date [FY26 10-K, p.59 (Item 11 index) and Items 10–14 cross-reference]. That means there is no Summary Compensation Table, no Compensation Discussion & Analysis, and no disclosed bonus/LTIP metric weights for either co-CEO in the data pool. The one hard, filed exception is an employment offer letter for the new CFO, filed as **Exhibit 10.15** to the FY26 10-K.

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| Co-CEO Clayton M. Magouyrk (since Sept 22, 2025) | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Not disclosed — internal promotion, no offer letter filed; 2026 Proxy not yet filed [FY26 10-K, Item 10] |
| Co-CEO Michael D. Sicilia (since Sept 22, 2025) | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Not disclosed — internal promotion, no offer letter filed; 2026 Proxy not yet filed [FY26 10-K, Item 10] |
| CFO Hilary Maxson (offer accepted Jan 2026, started Apr 6, 2026) | $950,000/yr | Target $2,500,000 at 100% target achievement under the "FY26 Executive Bonus Plan"; overall bonus capped at $5,000,000 (2x target); prorated for FY26 based on start date | Total target grant value $26,000,000: 80% ($20,800,000) time-based equity (employee's choice of 100% stock options, or 50% options / 50% RSUs, vesting 40%/30%/20%/10% over years 1–4) + 20% ($5,200,000) performance-based stock options (PSOs) vesting over a 3-year performance period ending May 31, 2028, "subject to the achievement of certain revenue metrics" | ~3% cash (base) / ~97% cash-plus-target-bonus-and-equity is equity-dominated: base $950K + target bonus $2.5M = $3.45M cash vs $26M target equity, i.e. roughly 88% of total target pay ($29.45M) is equity | [FY26 10-K, Exhibit 10.15 — Offer letter and employment agreement with Hilary Maxson, dated Jan 25, 2026 (offer letter itself dated Jan 19, 2026)] |
| Former CEO Safra A. Catz (CEO through Sept 22, 2025; Exec Vice Chair since) | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | Insufficient Data | FY2026 (partial-year, pre-transition) pay not disclosed in the pool; her FY2025 pay lived in the FY2025 Proxy Statement, which is outside this run's data pool and outside the partial-data rule's scope for this report | Not in pool |

**The one concrete, filed data point:** the CFO's performance-based equity — 20% of her total target grant, or $5.2M in target value — is explicitly tied to **"revenue metrics,"** not a per-share or returns metric [Exhibit 10.15]. Note 11 (Stock-Based Compensation Plans) of the FY26 10-K separately confirms company-wide PSOs "vest over tranches probable of achievement" based on "performance-metric achievement," without naming the metric for the broader program [FY26 10-K, Note 11]. Nothing in the pool discloses the CEOs' pay structure at all, and nothing discloses the specific metrics or weights inside the FY26 Executive Bonus Plan itself (only its dollar target and cap for one executive).

| CFO time-based equity (80% of grant) | Continued employment only (4-year graded vesting 40/30/20/10) — a retention mechanism, not a performance test | 80% of total equity value | Not a performance metric at all — service-based | [Exhibit 10.15] |
| CFO performance stock options (PSOs, 20% of grant) | "Certain revenue metrics" over a 3-year performance period ending May 31, 2028 | 20% of total equity value ($5.2M target) | **Size-based** (revenue) — not per-share, not a returns metric (no ROIC/ROCE/EPS/FCF-per-share/TSR reference disclosed) | [Exhibit 10.15] |
| Company-wide PSO program (all grantees) | Described only generically as "performance targets" / "performance-metric achievement," reassessed each interim period | Not disclosed | Insufficient Data (cannot classify without the specific metric) | [FY26 10-K, Note 11 — Stock-Based Compensation Plans] |

The single concrete metric this pool discloses — the CFO's PSO revenue target — is a **size-based** metric under the module's own classification rule (revenue, absolute EBITDA, deal count, and headcount are size-based; ROIC, ROCE, EPS, FCF-per-share, and TSR are per-share/returns-based). No per-share or returns-based metric appears anywhere in the disclosed record.

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| Signal | Detail | Source |

| Pay vs peers | **Web-sourced, unverified, dated 2026-08-14:** median S&P tech CEO total direct compensation runs roughly $25M–$40M+, and at large-cap tech companies overall equity typically makes up ~57% of total direct pay, with relative TSR now the dominant long-term metric at roughly 70% of S&P 500 companies (peer-context only — not Oracle-specific, and not a substitute for Oracle's own disclosed figures) [Web: general tech-executive-compensation benchmarking summary, accessed 2026-08-14 (unverified)]. Against that generic band, the CFO's ~$29.5M target sits inside the range typically reserved for a CEO, not a CFO — a magnitude flag even without an Oracle-specific CFO peer figure | Web-sourced (unverified) |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | Cannot be assessed for either co-CEO (no disclosure at all). For the CFO, only 20% of the equity grant ($5.2M of $26M) carries any performance test, and that test is revenue (size), not a per-share or margin/cash metric; the other 80% of equity and the entire base salary are time- or service-based, not performance-conditioned | [Exhibit 10.15] |

A clean data point, though it says nothing about whether the underlying metrics reward per-share value | [FY26 10-K, cover page checkboxes] |

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

Oracle's own pay disclosure for this fiscal year is almost entirely absent — the 2026 Proxy Statement that would show both co-CEOs' bonus and LTIP metrics, weights, and dollar totals has not been filed, so alignment cannot be judged at the CEO level at all. The one hard number the pool does give — the new CFO's performance-based stock options, 20% of a $26 million target equity grant, vesting on "certain revenue metrics" rather than any per-share, margin, cash-flow, or returns test — is a size-based metric, and it sits inside a year in which Oracle's levered free cash flow turned sharply negative (−$24.5bn) and total debt rose 54% while capex ran roughly $15bn ahead of what management had guided one quarter earlier [`01_management-and-track-record.md`, Section 4]; paying even part of one senior executive's package on revenue growth, in that specific context, points the same direction as the spending pattern already visible in the numbers rather than against it. Given the near-total absence of proxy disclosure, the honest classification is **"mixed"** — not because the visible evidence is balanced, but because there is too little of it to call outright misaligned, and what little exists (a revenue-based PSO metric, a large equity-heavy CFO package with no per-share performance gate, no say-on-pay data, no CEO disclosure at all) leans toward size rather than per-share value without proving a pattern.

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — ORCL

## 1. Ownership

Ellison (Co-Founder, Executive Chair & CTO) | 40.21% (1,158,232,353 shares) | Single class common stock | Granted/earned — accumulated almost entirely through exercise-and-retain of stock options over the 2015–2026 record; no open-market cash purchases found in the pool | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Top Holders table]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls, Ellison rows FY2016–FY2026] |
| Other insiders/directors (aggregate, per the "Individuals/Insiders" ownership class) | 40.45% of total shares outstanding (1,165,033,074 shares) — the ~0.24-point gap above Ellison's 40.21% is the combined stake of ~20 other insiders/former insiders in this class (Henley 0.06%, Screven 0.10%, Catz 0.04%, and smaller director/officer holdings) | Single class common stock | Mixed — director stakes accumulate through annual RSU/option grants ("Derivative Exercise and Retained Stock" entries), not cash purchases; two small, genuine open-market cash buys are on record for directors (Moorman, Fairhead) but they are immaterial in size | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Ownership Summary table]; [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership History.xls, History sheet] |

Ellison is a large, engaged founder-shareholder-director but is Executive Chair/CTO, not CEO; the operating CEO role sits with two non-founder co-CEOs (internal promotions) | Single class common stock | N/A | [`management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md`, Section 1] |

Total insider ownership (Individuals/Insiders class, per the ownership-summary vendor export): **40.45% of total shares outstanding** (1,165,033,074 of 2,880,471,000 shares as of the position dates in the export, market value $178,576.3mm at the $145.48 close on Aug-12-2026) [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Ownership Summary table]. Ellison alone accounts for essentially all of it (40.21 of the 40.45 points). Institutions hold 44.01% (1,267,654,925 shares); Public and Other holds 15.40%.

**Bought vs. granted, in full.** Across the entire Form-4 history captured in the pool (2009–2026), neither Ellison nor Catz has a single **open-market cash purchase** on record. Ellison's share count has grown almost entirely through "Derivative Exercise and Retained Stock" transactions — he exercises vested stock options (paying the strike price in cash, e.g. $51.13/share on the July 2025 tranches) and keeps the resulting shares rather than selling them, which is a genuine cash outlay but is compensation-linked, not an open-market purchase decision made with disposable cash [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls, Ellison rows]. The handful of true open-market **buys** in the whole insider record belong to independent directors in small dollar amounts (e.g., Moorman IV, $947,913 in Feb-2025; Fairhead, $112,258 in Jul-2025) — none from the Executive Chair, the co-CEOs, or the CFO seat.

Aggregate trailing-12-month snapshot (per the vendor's own summary table, most recent 3-month slice): **May-13-2026 to Aug-13-2026 — 6 buy transactions totaling 12,546 shares vs. 3 sell transactions totaling 400,138 shares** [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Ownership Activity table]. Over the fuller 12-month window covered above (Aug-2025–Aug-2026), every transaction with a named senior executive (both co-CEOs, the outgoing bridge-CFO-turned-EVP, the Chief Accounting Officer, the Chief Legal Officer, and the Executive Vice Chairman) is a **sale** — either an open-market disposition or an option-exercise-and-immediate-sale. **No open-market purchases by any named executive officer or the Executive Chair are on record in the trailing 12 months.**

**Net insider activity: net selling.** Distinguishing the transaction types: the large-dollar Ellison and Catz transactions from the trailing period on record ($332mm and $307mm option exercises in July 2025, retained not sold) predate the strict 12-month window used here but establish the pattern — option exercises are typically either fully retained (building the founder stake further, funded by cash paid at strike) or exercised-and-sold in the same trade (monetizing, not accumulating). Within the strict trailing-12-month window, every dollar-material transaction by a named officer is a sale. The mechanical, same-day, price-laddered execution seen in Henley's, Sicilia's, and Levey's trades is consistent with pre-scheduled Rule 10b5-1 trading arrangements, which Oracle's insider trading policy explicitly permits for the Board and EVP-and-above tier [FY26 10-K, "Rule 10b5-1 Trading Plans"], but the pool's Form-4 export does not carry a field tagging which specific trades ran under a 10b5-1 plan — this is inferred from the pattern, not confirmed line-by-line, and is labeled accordingly.

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|

**No pledging disclosed.** Oracle's own Insider Trading Policy, filed as an exhibit to the FY26 10-K, states: "the Pledging Policy prohibits applicable employees from holding Oracle securities in a margin account or otherwise pledging Oracle securities as collateral for a loan," with limited exceptions requiring Chief Legal Officer pre-approval [FY26 10-K, "Margin Accounts and Pledging Oracle Securities"]. This is a genuine, cited disclosure — not an inference from silence. The Ownership Summary, Ownership History, and Insider Trading workbooks in the pool contain zero mentions of pledged/encumbered shares for Ellison or any other insider [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf; Public Ownership History.xls; Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls]. Consistent with `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan.md`, which independently checked and cleared this item (computed ratio "not measurable / effectively 0%, well under the 50% threshold"). No RF-OWN-001/002 red flag is fired.

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | None found. Oracle has a single class of common stock, one share/one vote; no dual-class or super-voting share structure is described anywhere in the FY26 10-K | [FY26 10-K, cover page and capital-structure disclosure; no dual-class language found on targeted search] |
| Controlled-company status | Not a formally "controlled company" under NYSE listing rules (that designation requires a single holder or group with >50% of voting power) — Ellison at 40.21% is a large, engaged plurality holder but not a majority holder. No other insider or bloc approaches a controlling stake | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf, Top Holders table] |
| Shareholder bloc with board-nomination or veto rights | None disclosed in the pool. No shareholder agreement, standstill, or board-designation-rights provision for Ellison or any other holder was found in the 10-K or the ownership exports | [FY26 10-K; Public Ownership Summary.rtf] — absence of a provision, not independently confirmed against the proxy (DEF 14A not in the pool) |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | Ellison is the company's own founder and remains an active, engaged Executive Chair and CTO with essentially all of his wealth concentrated in Oracle stock (40.21%, ~$168.5bn at the position date). This is the opposite profile of the three structurally-conflicted owner types the filter targets (government control, listed-subsidiary-of-a-parent, sprawling unrelated conglomerate) — a large founder stake concentrated in a single operating business is a classic alignment signal, not a structural cap | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Summary.rtf]; [`management-governance/01_management-and-track-record.md`, Section 1] |

**Unaligned-owner test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 6):** Does not apply. Oracle is not government-controlled, not a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, and not a sprawling unrelated-diversified conglomerate — it is a single-business operating company where the largest shareholder is the founder who built it and still runs product/technology strategy day to day (as CTO). **RF-OWN-004 is not triggered.** The relevant governance question for Oracle is not owner misalignment but the separate finding (already carried in `01_management-and-track-record.md`) that Ellison retains outsized influence (40.21% of the vote, plus the Chair and CTO seats) over a board and management team that also includes two non-founder co-CEOs — a founder-influence concentration risk, distinct from the §24 Filter 6 unaligned-owner test.

## 4A. Market Conduct

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Insider buys/sells vs subsequent results (timing) | Every named-executive Form 4 in the trailing 12 months is a sale (Section 2). The two largest single-day executive sale clusters landed Sept 19–23, 2025 (Sicilia $26.96mm, Magouyrk $15.57mm — the same week the co-CEO transition and new segment structure were announced, Sept 22, 2025) and June 24, 2026 (Henley $47.29mm — two weeks after the Jun 10, 2026 Q4 FY26 print that raised FY2027 guidance). Neither cluster precedes a subsequently-disclosed *negative* surprise in the pool's earnings-consensus record; the September sales followed the CEO transition announcement rather than preceding a weak result, and no adverse announcement follows the June 2026 sales in the pool through the Aug-14-2026 as-of date | [Insider Trading tab]; [`earnings/04_guidance-consensus.md`, Section 6] |
| Unusual price / volume before announcements | Not found in the pool. No exchange-surveillance disclosure, unusual-activity flag, or pre-announcement volume-spike analysis is present in any pool source | Not available — genuine data gap, not investigated further within this module's source set |
| Exchange clarification sought for a price move | Not found in the pool. No NYSE inquiry or company clarification statement is present in the Key Developments log or 10-K | Not available |
| Institutional (FII/DII/MF) holding trend; any sharp high-quality-holder exit | Institutional flows for the most recent tracked quarter (Dec-31-2025 to Mar-31-2026): 210 new positions (+191.9mm sh), 1,571 increased (+64.7mm sh), 1,502 decreased (-82.5mm sh), 452 sold out (-187.3mm sh) — net roughly flat to slightly negative on share count, against a backdrop of heavy gross churn. The single largest nominal "seller," The Vanguard Group, Inc. (shares to 0, a -174.8mm-share change), is materially explained by an intra-family reclassification: two new Vanguard entities (Vanguard Capital Management, LLC and Vanguard Portfolio Management, LLC) appear for the first time in the same quarter holding 120.9mm and 56.7mm shares respectively — a combined 177.6mm shares, almost exactly offsetting the "Vanguard Group" outflow. This reads as an internal Vanguard reporting-entity change, not a genuine index/active exit, though the pool does not confirm this explicitly. Genuine reductions: JP Morgan Asset Management (-8.75mm sh), FMR LLC/Fidelity (-6.46mm sh), Coatue Management (sold out entirely, -4.44mm sh), BMO Asset Management (-3.82mm sh) | [Oracle Corporation NYSE ORCL Public Ownership Insider Trading.xls, Ownership Activity and Top Buyers/Sellers tables] |

No trade-timing red flag is fired: the pool does not show insider selling immediately preceding a subsequently disclosed weak print, and the September 2025 and June 2026 sale clusters both sit around routine, publicly-known catalysts (a scheduled leadership transition and a scheduled earnings call) rather than ahead of undisclosed bad news. This is a negative finding (absence of the pattern), not an affirmative clearance — the pool does not include exchange-surveillance data that would independently confirm no unusual pre-announcement trading occurred.

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

Insider skin in the game is real but almost entirely option-derived, not cash-bought on the open market: Ellison's 40.21% stake (worth roughly $168.5bn) grew through decades of exercise-and-retain option activity rather than a single disclosed open-market purchase, and no other named executive has bought shares with cash in the trailing 12 months either. The trailing-12-month insider signal is unambiguous net selling — every dollar-material Form 4 from the co-CEOs, the Executive Vice Chairman, the Chief Accounting Officer, and the Chief Legal Officer is a disposal, mechanically laddered in a way consistent with (but not confirmed as) pre-scheduled 10b5-1 plans, so this reads as routine liquidity monetization rather than a conviction signal in either direction. The biggest control-related fact for minority holders is Ellison's 40.21% vote concentrated with an active Executive Chair/CTO role sitting alongside a newly-installed non-founder co-CEO structure — not a pledging or encumbrance risk (pledging is company-policy-prohibited and none is disclosed), but a real concentration-of-influence factor to track once the 2026 proxy is available to confirm formal board-designation rights, if any.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

# Board & Shareholder Rights — ORCL

## 0. Data Availability Note

Oracle's FY26 10-K incorporates Part III (Items 10–14: directors, executive compensation, security ownership, related-party transactions, and principal accountant fees) **entirely by reference** to the "2026 Proxy Statement," which the company states it will file with the SEC within 120 days of its May 31, 2026 fiscal year end [FY26 10-K, Part III, Items 10–14]. As of this report's date (2026-08-14), that 2026 proxy has not yet been filed, and no DEF 14A / proxy document of any vintage is in `data/ORCL/`. This is a genuine primary-source gap, not a language issue (CLAUDE.md §27) — the document simply does not exist yet for the current fiscal year.

Per the Partial-Data Rule, this agent used the pool's FY26 10-K signature page (which names the sitting board) and labeled, dated **web sources** — principally Oracle's most recently filed proxy (FY2025 DEF 14A, filed 2025-09-26, covering the board elected at the November 18, 2025 annual meeting) sourced via SEC EDGAR, Oracle's own bylaws/governance-guidelines filings, and the FY2025 annual-meeting voting-results 8-K — to fill governance facts not in the pool. These are CLAUDE.md §4 tier-7 web sources (search-engine syntheses of primary SEC filings, not a direct read of the filing text, since direct fetch of sec.gov returned HTTP 403). They are dated, labeled "Web (unverified)" throughout, and confidence is capped at 2–3 accordingly. Where a fact could not be found in any source, it is marked "Insufficient Data." The 2026 proxy, once filed, should be used to confirm or update every web-sourced fact below.

Henley (Executive Vice Chair, CEO/Chairman 1991–2004), Clayton M. Magouyrk (Co-CEO), Michael D. Sicilia (Co-CEO). Independent: Awo Ablo, Jeffrey S. Berg, Michael J. Boskin, Bruce R. Chizen, Rona A.
